At $n=1$ the sole term is $f(1)\varepsilon(1)=f(1)$. This gives a useful way to check <Dirichlet convolution> calculations: inserting $\varepsilon$ leaves every value unchanged. The three <arithmetic functions> $\mathbf1$, $\operatorname{id}$, and $\varepsilon$ all take value $1$ at $1$, but at $12$ the <constant-one arithmetic function>, the <identity arithmetic function>, and the <identity for Dirichlet convolution> take values $1$, $12$, and $0$, respectively. https://darijgrinberg.gitlab.io/floor.pdf[Darij Grinberg, MIT 18.781 lecture notes, Theorem 2.3.4]{ref}

The <Möbius function> is the inverse of the <constant-one arithmetic function> under <Dirichlet convolution>, meaning $\mu*\mathbf1=\mathbf1*\mu=\varepsilon$. The <Möbius divisor-sum identity> gives a short proof: for $n>1$ the nonzero terms expand $\prod_{p\mid n}(1-1)=0$, while at $1$ the sum is $\mu(1)=1$. At $12$, the computation is
$$
(\mu*\mathbf1)(12)=\mu(1)+\mu(2)+\mu(3)+\mu(4)+\mu(6)+\mu(12)=1-1-1+0+1+0=0=\varepsilon(12).
$$
The zeros occur because $4$ and $12$ are divisible by the square of the <prime number> $2$. Consequently, if $F=f*\mathbf1$ is a <divisor sum>, then <associativity> gives $F*\mu=f*(\mathbf1*\mu)=f*\varepsilon=f$: this is <Möbius inversion>.

= Dirichlet hyperbola method
{c}
{parent=Dirichlet convolution}
{wiki}

The <Dirichlet hyperbola method> turns a long <summation> over a <Dirichlet convolution> into two short ones by covering the <lattice points> below a <hyperbola> with two overlapping strips. Its simplest application is an exact count: for a <positive integer> $N$, every positive <integer divisor> $a$ of $n\le N$ corresponds to the <ordered pair> $(a,n/a)$. Thus the <divisor summatory function> counts precisely the <ordered pairs> of <positive integers> with $ab\le N$:
$$
D(N)=\sum_{n=1}^N\tau(n)
=\#\{(a,b)\in\mathbb Z_{>0}^2:ab\le N\}
=\sum_{a=1}^N\left\lfloor\frac Na\right\rfloor.
$$
Here $\tau$ is the <divisor function>, and the <floor function> gives the number of allowed values of $b$ for each $a$. The axes are excluded, while points on $ab=N$ are included.

Set $r=\lfloor\sqrt N\rfloor$, using the <floor function> and the positive <square root>. **Every counted pair has $a\le r$ or $b\le r$.** Otherwise $ab\ge(r+1)^2>N$. The first strip contains $\sum_{a=1}^r\lfloor N/a\rfloor$ points; swapping the two coordinates gives the same count for the second. Their overlap contains all $r^2$ pairs with $1\le a,b\le r$, since $r^2\le N$. The <inclusion-exclusion principle> therefore gives the exact identity
$$
\boxed{D(N)=2\sum_{a=1}^r\left\lfloor\frac Na\right\rfloor-r^2,
\qquad r=\lfloor\sqrt N\rfloor.}
$$
The subtraction is for the whole overlap square, rather than only the diagonal. The same <Dirichlet hyperbola method> identity holds for real $x\ge1$, with $D(x)=\sum_{n\le x}\tau(n)$ and $r=\lfloor\sqrt x\rfloor$. <Multiplicative number theory: The pretentious approach by Andrew Granville and K. Soundararajan>[Andrew Granville and K. Soundararajan, Multiplicative number theory: The pretentious approach, §1.1.4, pp. 7–8: divisor pairing and the hyperbola split]{ref}

\Image[dirichlet-hyperbola-lattice-split.png]
{title=The <Dirichlet hyperbola method> counts 35 positive <lattice points> with $ab\le12$: two strips of 22 points overlap in a square of nine points}

= Divisor summatory function
{parent=Dirichlet hyperbola method}
{title2=$D(x)=\sum_{n\le x}\tau(n)$}
{wiki}

The <divisor summatory function> adds the <divisor function> up to a cutoff $x\ge1$. It counts all <ordered pairs> of <positive integers> whose product is at most $x$. For $N=12$, the twelve individual <divisor function> values are
$$
(\tau(1),\ldots,\tau(12))=(1,2,2,3,2,4,2,4,3,4,2,6),
\qquad D(12)=35.
$$
Counting columns of <lattice points> instead gives $12+6+4+3+2+2+1+1+1+1+1+1=35$. The <Dirichlet hyperbola method> needs only the first three column counts:
$$
D(12)=2(12+6+4)-3^2=44-9=35.
$$
The <arithmetic work of the Dirichlet hyperbola method> is much smaller even though the answer is identical.

This is useful for elementary averages: individual <divisor function> values fluctuate, but their <summation> has the regular behavior given by the <divisor summatory asymptotic>. Dividing that estimate by a <positive integer> $N$ gives
$$
\frac1N\sum_{n=1}^N\tau(n)
=\log N+2\gamma-1+O(N^{-1/2})\qquad(N\longrightarrow\infty),
$$
where $\log$ is the <natural logarithm>, $\gamma$ is the <Euler-Mascheroni constant>, and $O$ uses <big O notation>. This describes an average, rather than an estimate for every individual <divisor function> value. <Multiplicative number theory: The pretentious approach by Andrew Granville and K. Soundararajan>[§1.1.4, p. 8, equation (1.1.13): the divisor summatory asymptotic]{ref}

= Asymmetric Dirichlet hyperbola split
{parent=Dirichlet hyperbola method}
{title2=$AB=N$}

A balanced <Dirichlet hyperbola method> split takes real thresholds $A=B=\sqrt N$. An asymmetric split takes $1\le A\le N$ and $B=N/A$. Every <lattice point> with $ab\le N$ then has $a\le A$ or $b\le B$, and every point in their overlap rectangle is allowed because $AB=N$. For <arithmetic functions> $f,g$, define their <partial sums> by $F(t)=\sum_{a\le t}f(a)$ and $G(t)=\sum_{b\le t}g(b)$, always over <positive integers>. The same <inclusion-exclusion principle>, now with weight $f(a)g(b)$, yields
$$
\sum_{n\le N}(f*g)(n)
=\sum_{a\le A}f(a)G(N/a)
+\sum_{b\le B}g(b)F(N/b)-F(A)G(B),
\qquad AB=N.
$$
No <multiplicativity> assumption is needed: this is a finite rearrangement of <Dirichlet convolution>. The <constant-one arithmetic function> in both positions recovers the <divisor summatory function>. For $N=12$, $A=2$, $B=6$ gives
$$
D(12)=(12+6)+(12+6+4+3+2+2)-2\cdot6=18+29-12=35.
$$
It uses two columns and six rows, with a twelve-point overlap.

For general positive real thresholds, **$AB\ge N$ is sufficient for coverage**, since $a>A$ and $b>B$ would imply $ab>AB\ge N$. If $AB>N$, however, the overlap can include points above the <hyperbola>. The correct subtraction is then $\sum_{a\le A,\ b\le B,\ ab\le N}f(a)g(b)$; the product $F(A)G(B)$ is justified only when the whole integer rectangle lies below it. For example, $N=12$, $A=B=4$ covers every allowed pair, but its rectangle contains the forbidden pair $(4,4)$, so subtracting all sixteen points would give $34$ instead of $35$.

Conversely, the balanced integer thresholds $r,r$ have $r^2\le N$ and still cover every allowed pair because $(r+1)^2>N$. More generally, positive integer thresholds $U,V$ give coverage exactly when $(U+1)(V+1)>N$, and their overlap is a full rectangle exactly when $UV\le N$. These discrete conditions explain why the square-root identity works even when $r^2<N$.

= Arithmetic work of the Dirichlet hyperbola method
{parent=Dirichlet hyperbola method}

The direct <divisor summatory function> formula evaluates $N$ floor divisions. Its balanced <Dirichlet hyperbola method> formula evaluates $r=\lfloor\sqrt N\rfloor$ floor divisions and reuses their <sum> for the other strip, followed by doubling and square-overlap subtraction. Thus it uses $O(\sqrt N)$ <arithmetic operations>, with <big O notation>, in a model that counts each division, addition and multiplication as one operation. At $N=10^6$, the division counts for these two formulas are $10^6$ and $10^3$.

\Image[dirichlet-hyperbola-work-and-partial-sums.png]
{title=The balanced <Dirichlet hyperbola method> reduces floor divisions from $N$ to $\lfloor\sqrt N\rfloor$; for $N=12$, two sequences of <partial sums> reach the same exact answer 35}

For a general <Dirichlet convolution>, the asymmetric formula has $\lfloor A\rfloor+\lfloor B\rfloor$ outer terms; evaluating $F$ and $G$ has an additional cost. Under $AB=N$, equal thresholds minimize $A+B$, but an asymmetric split can be useful if the two <partial sums> have different evaluation costs. The balanced <divisor summatory function> gains the further reuse because its two <arithmetic functions> are identical.

These are <arithmetic operation> counts, rather than counts of elementary operations on <binary digits>: the <integers> grow with $N$, and division of longer <integers> costs more in that model of <computational complexity theory>. Computing $r$ also requires an exact integer <square root>, or checking and correcting a numerical <square root> until $r^2\le N<(r+1)^2$ holds.

= Divisor summatory asymptotic
{parent=Dirichlet hyperbola method}
{title2=$\sum_{n\le x}\tau(n)=x\log x+(2\gamma-1)x+O(\sqrt x)$}

The <Dirichlet hyperbola method> gives $D(x)=2\sum_{a\le r}\lfloor x/a\rfloor-r^2$, where $r=\lfloor\sqrt x\rfloor$. The <harmonic number> estimate $H_r=\log r+\gamma+O(1/r)$, with $\gamma$ the <Euler's constant>, yields $D(x)=2x(\log r+\gamma)-r^2+O(x/r+r)$. Substitution of $r=\sqrt x+O(1)$ gives the displayed <asymptotic expansion>. Comparison of $\sum_{n\le x}\log n$ with its integral consequently gives $\sum_{n\le N}(\tau(n)-\log n-2\gamma)=O(\sqrt N)$ for integer $N$.

= Harmonic divisor sum
{parent=Dirichlet hyperbola method}

Write $B(t)=\sum_{n\leq t}\tau(n)/n$ and $r=\lfloor\sqrt t\rfloor$. The weighted <Dirichlet hyperbola method> gives
$$
B(t)=2\sum_{a\leq r}\frac{H_{\lfloor t/a\rfloor}}a-H_r^2
=\tfrac12\log^2t+2\gamma\log t+c+O\left(\frac{\log(2t)}{\sqrt t}\right).
$$
Indeed, use $H_{\lfloor u\rfloor}=\log u+\gamma+O(1/u)$ and $\sum_{a\leq r}(\log a)/a=\tfrac12\log^2r+c\prime+O(\log(2r)/r)$. The latter follows by comparing each summand with the integral on its unit interval: the derivative of $(\log t)/t$ has an integrable tail. Since $r=\sqrt t+O(1)$, substitution yields the expansion. Here $H_n$ is the <harmonic number> and $\gamma$ the <Euler-Mascheroni constant>.

= Divisor function
{title2=$\tau(n)$}
{parent=Arithmetic function}
{wiki}

The <divisor function> $\tau(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}1$ counts the positive <integer divisors> of a <positive integer> $n$. In particular $\tau(1)=1$, because $1$ has just one positive <integer divisor>. More generally the notation $\sigma_k(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}d^k$ gives $\tau=\sigma_0$ and the <sum-of-divisors function> $\sigma_1$. Thus the <divisor function> counts the positive <integer divisors>, while $\sigma_1$ adds their sizes. https://darijgrinberg.gitlab.io/floor.pdf[Darij Grinberg, MIT 18.781 lecture notes, Definition 2.1.2 and Theorem 2.1.6]{ref}

If the <prime factorization> is $n=\prod_{k=1}^r p_k^{a_k}$ with distinct <prime numbers> $p_k$ and positive exponents $a_k$, then
$$
\tau(n)=\prod_{k=1}^r(a_k+1).
$$
The short proof is a count: each positive <integer divisor> has a unique form $\prod_{k=1}^r p_k^{b_k}$, where $0\le b_k\le a_k$. There are $a_k+1$ choices at each <prime number>, and choosing them gives every positive <integer divisor> exactly once. For $n=1$ there are no <prime factors> and the product is $1$.

For $12=2^2\cdot3$, choose $i\in\{0,1,2\}$ and $j\in\{0,1\}$ to obtain $2^i3^j$. The six values are $1,2,4,3,6,12$; sorted, the positive <integer divisors> are $1,2,3,4,6,12$. Therefore $\tau(12)=(2+1)(1+1)=6$. The diagram makes the independent exponent choices visible.

\Image[divisor-count-prime-exponents.png]
{title=The <prime factorization> $12=2^2\cdot3$ gives a three-by-two array of positive <integer divisors> $2^i3^j$, so the <divisor function> has $\tau(12)=6$}

This count is useful because a known <prime factorization> gives $\tau(n)$ without testing every smaller <positive integer> for <divisibility>. It also proves the <divisor function> is a <multiplicative function>: <coprime integers> have disjoint <prime factors>, so their exponent choices combine. For example $\tau(6)=4=\tau(2)\tau(3)$, but $\tau(4)=3\ne4=\tau(2)^2$, so it is not a <completely multiplicative function>. Equivalently, $\tau=\mathbf1*\mathbf1$ under <Dirichlet convolution>, counting <ordered pairs> of <positive integers> whose product is $n$.

= Subpower bound for the divisor function
{parent=Divisor function}
{title2=$\tau(n)\ll_\eta n^\eta\quad(\eta>0)$}

For each $\eta>0$, the <divisor function> satisfies the displayed bound. Write $n=\prod p^{a_p}$, so $\tau(n)=\prod(a_p+1)$. For all sufficiently large <primes>, $a+1\le2^a\le p^{\eta a}$ for every $a\ge0$. For each of the finitely many smaller <primes>, $\sup_{a\ge0}(a+1)p^{-\eta a}$ is finite. Multiplying these finitely many constants proves the bound. Products of a fixed number of divisor functions obey the same conclusion after reducing $\eta$.

= Divisor-square summatory bound
{parent=Divisor function}
{title2=$\sum_{n\le X}d(n)^2\ll X\log^3(2X)$}

The <divisor function> satisfies $\sum_{n\le X}d(n)^2\ll X\log^3(2X)$. <Partial summation> consequently gives $\sum_{n\le X}d(n)^2/n\ll\log^4(2X)$.

= Von Mangoldt function
{c}
{title2=$\Lambda(n)$}
{parent=Arithmetic function}
{wiki}

The <Von Mangoldt function> assigns a <natural logarithm> weight to each positive power of a <prime number>. For a <positive integer> $n$,
$$
\Lambda(n)=\begin{cases}
\log p,&n=p^k,\quad p\text{ prime},\ k\geq1,\\
0,&\text{otherwise}.
\end{cases}
$$
Here and throughout the <Chebyshev functions>, **$\log$ means the <natural logarithm>**. In particular, $\Lambda(1)=0$: the exponent must be positive, even though the convention in <prime power> allows exponent zero. The <support of a function> of $\Lambda$ therefore consists precisely of the <prime powers> greater than one. <254A – analytic prime number theory/254A, Notes 1: Elementary multiplicative number theory>{ref}

For example, $\Lambda(2)=\Lambda(4)=\Lambda(8)=\log2$ and $\Lambda(3)=\Lambda(9)=\log3$, whereas $\Lambda(6)=\Lambda(12)=0$. A <composite number> can have a nonzero weight: $8$ has just one distinct <prime factor>. But $12=2^2\cdot3$ has two distinct <prime factors>, so its weight is zero. At $p^k$ the weight is **$\log p$, not $\log(p^k)=k\log p$**. This choice lets successive powers record successive copies of the same <prime factor> in the <Von Mangoldt divisor identity>.

The <Von Mangoldt function> is useful because it connects two views of the <prime numbers>: the <prime factorization> of an individual <integer>, through a <divisor sum>, and the accumulated weights below a cutoff, through the <second Chebyshev function>. The latter turns questions about the distribution of <prime numbers> into questions about a <partial sum> of an <arithmetic function>.

= Selberg symmetry formula
{parent=Von Mangoldt function}
{c}

The elementary Selberg symmetry formula can be written
$$
\psi(x)+\frac1{\log x}\sum_{n\leq x}\Lambda(n)\psi(x/n)=2x+O\left(\frac{x}{\log x}\right).
$$
Use the weights $\lambda_d=\mu(d)\log(x/d)/\log x$. Their <divisor sum> is $1_{n=1}+\Lambda(n)/\log x$. Apply them to the <integrated Chebyshev sum>, then use the <Möbius harmonic logarithmic moments>. The <Chebyshev estimate> shows this is equivalent, with an $O(x)$ error, to $\sum_{n\leq x}\Lambda(n)\log n+\sum_{mn\leq x}\Lambda(m)\Lambda(n)=2x\log x+O(x)$.

= Von Mangoldt divisor identity
{c}
{parent=Von Mangoldt function}
{title2=$\sum_{d\mid n}\Lambda(d)=\log n$}

For every <positive integer> $n$, the <divisor sum> of its <Von Mangoldt function> weights recovers its <natural logarithm>:
$$
\sum_{d\mid n}\Lambda(d)=\log n.
$$
The sum runs over the positive <integer divisors> of $n$. For $n=1$, its only term is $\Lambda(1)=0=\log1$. Equivalently, if $L(n)=\log n$ and $\mathbf1$ is the <constant-one arithmetic function>, then $\mathbf1*\Lambda=L$, where $*$ is <Dirichlet convolution>. <254A – analytic prime number theory/254A, Notes 1: Elementary multiplicative number theory>{ref}

For $12=2^2\cdot3$, the positive <integer divisors> are $1,2,3,4,6,12$. Only $2,3,4$ contribute:
$$
\Lambda(1)+\Lambda(2)+\Lambda(3)+\Lambda(4)+\Lambda(6)+\Lambda(12)
=0+\log2+\log3+\log2+0+0
=\log12.
$$
Thus the vanishing of $\Lambda(12)$ loses no information here: the <divisor sum> retrieves both copies of the <prime factor> $2$ from the <integer divisors> $2$ and $4$.

\Image[von-mangoldt-divisor-contributions.png]
{id=image-von-mangoldt-divisor-contributions}
{title=The <Von Mangoldt divisor identity> assembles $\log12$ from three nonzero divisor weights}
{description=The six positive <integer divisors> of $12$ appear in increasing order. Blue bars contribute $\log2$ at $2$ and $4$; the orange bar contributes $\log3$ at $3$. The remaining three weights are zero. The adjacent stacked bar shows the total $2\log2+\log3=\log12$.}

Proof outline: by the <fundamental theorem of arithmetic>, write $n=\prod_{i=1}^r p_i^{a_i}$ with distinct <prime numbers> $p_i$ and positive exponents $a_i$. Among the positive <integer divisors>, the ones with nonzero <Von Mangoldt function> weights are exactly $p_i,p_i^2,\ldots,p_i^{a_i}$. Consequently
$$
\sum_{d\mid n}\Lambda(d)=\sum_{i=1}^r\sum_{j=1}^{a_i}\log p_i
=\sum_{i=1}^r a_i\log p_i
=\log\!\left(\prod_{i=1}^r p_i^{a_i}\right)=\log n.
$$
The <natural logarithm> turns the <prime factorization> product into a sum, and the <prime powers> greater than one supply exactly the required multiplicities.

= Chebyshev function
{c}
{parent=Von Mangoldt function}
{wiki}
{title2=$\vartheta(x),\ \psi(x)$}

The two <Chebyshev functions> measure the total <natural logarithm> weight of <prime numbers> or of <prime powers> greater than one below a real cutoff $x\geq0$:
$$
\vartheta(x)=\sum_{p\leq x}\log p,
\qquad
\psi(x)=\sum_{n\leq x}\Lambda(n)
=\sum_{\substack{p^k\leq x\\k\geq1}}\log p.
$$
Here $p$ ranges over <prime numbers>, $n$ over <positive integers>, and $k$ over <positive integers>. The <Chebyshev theta function> $\vartheta$, also written $\theta$, weights each <prime number> once. The <second Chebyshev function> $\psi$ weights every positive power of each <prime number> separately. Both vanish for $0\leq x<2$; the inclusive cutoff makes them <right-continuous functions> and <nondecreasing functions>, constant between their jumps. <Elementary Number Theory/7.2: Chebyshev's Functions>{ref}

At $x=10$, the <prime numbers> are $2,3,5,7$, while the extra <prime powers> greater than one are $4,8,9$. Exactly,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\vartheta(10)&=\log2+\log3+\log5+\log7=\log210,\\
\psi(10)&=3\log2+2\log3+\log5+\log7=\log2520,\\
\psi(10)-\vartheta(10)&=2\log2+\log3=\log12.
\end{aligned}
$$
At each <prime number> $p$, both <Chebyshev functions> jump by $\log p$. At a <prime power> $p^k$ with $k\geq2$, only the <second Chebyshev function> jumps, by $\log p$. At $6$ and $10$ neither jumps. Notice that the gap is already positive at $5$ and $7$: a shared jump does not remove the earlier contribution from $4$.

\Image[chebyshev-prime-power-staircases.png]
{id=image-chebyshev-prime-power-staircases}
{title=The <Chebyshev functions> separate at the extra <prime powers> $4$, $8$, and $9$}
{description=The upper panel compares $\vartheta(x)$ and $\psi(x)$ for $0\leq x\leq10$. Solid endpoints include the jump at each contributing <integer>; open endpoints show the preceding value. The lower panel isolates $\psi(x)-\vartheta(x)$, which jumps by $\log2$ at $4$ and $8$ and by $\log3$ at $9$, reaching $\log12$. All logarithms are <natural logarithms>.}

Grouping the <prime powers> by their exponent gives, for $x\geq2$,
$$
\psi(x)=\sum_{k=1}^{\lfloor\log x/\log2\rfloor}\vartheta(x^{1/k}),
\qquad
\psi(x)-\vartheta(x)=\sum_{k=2}^{\lfloor\log x/\log2\rfloor}\vartheta(x^{1/k}).
$$
The <floor function> cutoff is exact: $p\geq2$ forces $2^k\leq x$ whenever $p^k\leq x$. For each exponent, $p^k\leq x$ is equivalent to $p\leq x^{1/k}$. An upper cutoff smaller than the lower one means an empty sum. <Elementary Number Theory/7.2: Chebyshev's Functions>{ref}

= Second Chebyshev function
{title2=$\psi(x)$}
{c}
{parent=Chebyshev function}

The <second Chebyshev function> is the <partial sum> of the <Von Mangoldt function> up to a real cutoff $x\geq0$:
$$
\psi(x)=\sum_{1\leq n\leq x}\Lambda(n).
$$
For example, $\psi(3)=\log6$, $\psi(4)=\log12$, and $\psi(4.5)=\log12$. At $4=2^2$ the jump has size $\log2$; between $4$ and $5$ no new <prime power> greater than one enters the sum. In contrast, the <Chebyshev theta function> stays at $\vartheta(4)=\log6$.

There is also an exact connection with the <least common multiple>. For $N=\lfloor x\rfloor\geq1$, each <prime number> $p\leq N$ occurs to the highest exponent $k$ for which $p^k\leq N$, both in that <least common multiple> and in the accumulated <Von Mangoldt function> weights. Thus
$$
\psi(x)=\log\operatorname{lcm}(1,2,\ldots,N).
$$
This explains the $\log2520$ value at $x=10$: the <least common multiple> is $2^3\cdot3^2\cdot5\cdot7$. The <Chebyshev theta function> instead gives the <natural logarithm> of the <primorial> $2\cdot3\cdot5\cdot7=210$. The additional <prime powers> retain the highest required multiplicities.

Why is this useful for the distribution of <prime numbers>? The <prime number theorem> has the equivalent forms
$$
\pi(x)\sim\frac{x}{\log x},\qquad\vartheta(x)\sim x,\qquad\psi(x)\sim x
\quad(x\longrightarrow\infty),
$$
where $\pi$ is the <prime-counting function> and $\sim$ denotes <asymptotic equivalence>. Thus an <arithmetic function> defined using <prime factorization> has average weight tending to one over long initial intervals of the <positive integers>. These are substantive <asymptotic equivalences>, not consequences of the definitions or the <Von Mangoldt divisor identity> alone. <254A – analytic prime number theory/254A, Notes 2: Complex-analytic multiplicative number theory>{ref}

The elementary reason the two <Chebyshev functions> have the same leading behavior is that their difference is small compared with $x$. Every additional <prime power> has base $p\leq\sqrt x$, and for each such <prime number> its total extra weight is at most $\log x$. There are at most $\lfloor\sqrt x\rfloor$ such bases, so
$$
0\leq\psi(x)-\vartheta(x)\leq\lfloor\sqrt x\rfloor\log x,
\qquad
\frac{\psi(x)-\vartheta(x)}x\longrightarrow0.
$$
This transfers $\psi(x)\sim x$ to $\vartheta(x)\sim x$, or conversely, once either is established. <Partial summation> then relates the <Chebyshev theta function> to the <prime-counting function>. The estimate controls the extra <prime powers>; proving the leading value itself is the harder <prime number theorem> problem.

= First integral of the Chebyshev function
{parent=Second Chebyshev function}
{title2=$\Psi_1(x)=\int_0^x\psi(u)\,du$}

For the <second Chebyshev function>, finite summation gives $\Psi_1(x)=\sum_{n\le x}(x-n)\Lambda(n)$. For $c>1$, Mellin inversion of the kernel $1/(s(s+1))$ gives
$$
\Psi_1(x)=\frac1{2\pi i}\int_{c-i\infty}^{c+i\infty}-\frac{\zeta'(s)}{\zeta(s)}\frac{x^{s+1}}{s(s+1)}\,ds.
$$
The extra denominator makes this smoothing useful: the resulting sum over <nontrivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function> is absolutely convergent. This integral differs from the <integrated Chebyshev sum>, which denotes a sum of the values $\psi(x/n)$.

= Smoothed explicit formula for the Chebyshev function
{parent=First integral of the Chebyshev function}
{title2=$\Psi_1(x)=x^2/2-\sum_\rho x^{\rho+1}/(\rho(\rho+1))+O(x)$}

For $x>1$, moving the Mellin contour past the <pole> at one, the <nontrivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function>, and the remaining poles gives
$$
\Psi_1(x)=\frac{x^2}{2}-\sum_\rho\frac{x^{\rho+1}}{\rho(\rho+1)}-x\log(2\pi)+\frac{\zeta'(-1)}{\zeta(-1)}-\sum_{k\ge1}\frac{x^{1-2k}}{2k(2k-1)}.
$$
Zeros are included with multiplicity. The <Riemann–von Mangoldt formula> makes the zero sum absolutely convergent. If every nontrivial zero has real part below one, division by $x^2$ and <dominated convergence> give $\Psi_1(x)\sim x^2/2$. Monotonicity of $\psi$ then recovers $\psi(x)\sim x$ by upper and lower difference quotients.

= Integrated Chebyshev sum
{parent=Second Chebyshev function}
{title2=$T(x)$}

For $\psi(t)=\sum_{m\leq t}\Lambda(m)$, finite rearrangement and the <Von Mangoldt divisor identity> give
$$
T(x)=\sum_{n\leq x}\psi(x/n)=\sum_{m\leq x}\log m=\log(\lfloor x\rfloor!).
$$
The <Stirling formula>, or integral comparison for the <logarithm>, gives $T(x)=x\log x-x+O(\log(x+1))$ for real $x\geq1$.

= Chebyshev estimate
{c}
{parent=Second Chebyshev function}

The Chebyshev estimate is
$$
\psi(x)\asymp x.
$$
It gives upper and lower bounds of the correct linear order without asserting the <prime number theorem> asymptotic $\psi(x)\sim x$.

= Chebyshev estimate from central binomial coefficients
{c}
{parent=Chebyshev estimate}

The central <binomial coefficient> satisfies $4^m/(2m+1)\leq\binom{2m}{m}\leq4^m$. Prime factors between $m$ and $2m$ give a dyadic upper bound for the <Chebyshev theta function>. The prime-power exponents in the factorial quotient are sums of zeros and ones, so the central coefficient gives a linear lower bound for the <second Chebyshev function>. Higher prime powers contribute only $O(\sqrt x\log x)$; hence the prime-counting function has upper and lower bounds of order $x/\log x$.

= Riemann–von Mangoldt explicit formula
{c}
{parent=Second Chebyshev function}

For $x>1$ away from a jump of $\psi$,
$$
\psi(x)=x-\sum_\rho\frac{x^\rho}{\rho}
-\log(2\pi)-\frac12\log(1-x^{-2}),
$$
where the sum over <nontrivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function> is taken symmetrically. A finite-height version has an explicit truncation error controlled by $x/T$ and the distance from $x$ to the nearest prime power.

= Truncated explicit formula for the second Chebyshev function
{parent=Riemann–von Mangoldt explicit formula}
{title2=$\psi(x)=x-\sum_{|\Im\rho|\le T}x^\rho/\rho+O(x\log^2(xT)/T+\log x)$}

For $2\le T\le x$, the <truncated Perron formula> and a contour shift give the displayed form of the <Riemann–von Mangoldt explicit formula>. The <pole> at one contributes $x$ and the <nontrivial zeros of the Riemann zeta function> contribute the finite sum. One can choose a separated height in $[T,T+1]$ and then restore $T$ using the <local zero count for the Riemann zeta function>. The error absorbs the half-weight discrepancy at a prime power. Outside this range of $T$, one needs the fuller error with the near-integer terms retained.

= Vaughan's identity
{c}
{parent=Von Mangoldt function}
{wiki}

= Vaughan identity
{c}
{synonym}

For thresholds $U,V$, write $\mu_{\leq U}(d)=\mu(d)1_{d\leq U}$ and similarly for the other truncations. Vaughan's identity is
$$
\Lambda
=\Lambda_{\leq V}
+\mu_{\leq U}*\log
-\mu_{\leq U}*\Lambda_{\leq V}*1
+\mu_{>U}*\Lambda_{>V}*1.
$$
It decomposes a sum weighted by $\Lambda$ into short, Type I, and Type II bilinear sums.

= Prime exponential-sum estimate from Vaughan identity
{parent=Vaughan's identity}
{title2=$|\sum_{n\leq N}\Lambda(n)e(\alpha n)|\ll(Nq^{-1/2}+N^{4/5}+(Nq)^{1/2})\log^4(2N)$}

For reduced $a/q$ with $|\alpha-a/q|\leq q^{-2}$ and $1\leq q\leq N$, the displayed bound follows by splitting <Vaughan identity> with parameters $U=V=N^{2/5}$. <Type I sums> use short geometric-sum bounds; <type II sums> use the <Cauchy-Schwarz inequality> and rational spacing to control bilinear cancellation. A <badly approximable number> has a Dirichlet approximant with $q\asymp N^{1/2}$, yielding $O(N^{4/5}\log^4N)$ and hence $O(N^{9/10})$.

// Target: analytic-number-theory.bigb

= Logarithmic coefficient bound in Vaughan identity
{parent=Vaughan's identity}
{title2=$|\sum_{cd=m,c\le U,d\le V}\mu(c)\Lambda(d)|\le\log m$}

The <Von Mangoldt function> is nonnegative and satisfies $\sum_{d\mid m}\Lambda(d)=\log m$. Therefore the middle short-divisor coefficient in the <Vaughan identity> has the displayed pointwise bound. Grouping its long-divisor term as $\mu_{>U}(m)b(n)$, with $b(n)=\sum_{d\mid n,d>V}\Lambda(d)$, gives $0\le b(n)\le\log n$. This grouping permits bounded-coefficient <Type II sums> after division by $\log N$.

= Vaughan identity for the Möbius function
{c}
{parent=Vaughan's identity}
{title2=$\mu=\mu_{\leq U}+\mu_{\leq V}-\mu_{\leq U}*\mu_{\leq V}*\mathbf1+\mu_{>U}*\mu_{>V}*\mathbf1$}

The displayed identity is exact for all positive integers and all positive cutoffs. Expand $(\mu-\mu_{\leq U})*(\mu-\mu_{\leq V})*\mathbf1$ and use $\mu*\mathbf1=\varepsilon$. The two truncated terms are short sums; the negative term is a <type I sum> over <divisors> at most $UV$; the last term is a <type II sum> with both factor variables larger than a cutoff. This form of the <Vaughan identity> is specific to Möbius weights.

= Type I–type II inverse principle for Möbius correlation
{parent=Vaughan identity for the Möbius function}

For $|f|\leq1$, if $|\sum_{n\leq X}\mu(n)f(n)|\geq\delta X$ and $U+V\leq\delta X/2$, the <Vaughan identity for the Möbius function> gives a <type I sum> or a <type II sum> of modulus at least $\delta X/4$. The first is $\sum_{d\leq UV}c_d\sum_{k\leq X/d}f(dk)$, with $c_d=\sum_{bc=d,b\leq U,c\leq V}\mu(b)\mu(c)$. The second is $\sum_{d>V,w>U,dw\leq X}a_d\mu(w)f(dw)$, with $a_d=\sum_{c\mid d,c>V}\mu(c)$. Both coefficient sequences are bounded by the <divisor function>. Thus correlation forces either detection by small-modulus periodic indicators or a large multiplicatively organized <bilinear sum>, without claiming that $f$ itself is literally periodic or multiplicative.

= Vaughan identity proof
{parent=Vaughan's identity}
{c}

For <Dirichlet convolution>, let $\mathbf1(n)=1$ and $\epsilon(n)=1_{n=1}$. <Prime factorization> gives $\mu*\mathbf1=\epsilon$ and $\Lambda*\mathbf1=\log$. With subscripts denoting truncation at $U,V$,
$$
\begin{aligned}
\mu*\log&=\Lambda,\\
\mu_{>U}*\mathbf1*\Lambda_{>V}
&=(\epsilon-\mu_{\leq U}*\mathbf1)*\Lambda_{>V}\\
&=\Lambda_{>V}-\mu_{\leq U}*\log+\mu_{\leq U}*\mathbf1*\Lambda_{\leq V}.
\end{aligned}
$$
Rearranging proves the <Vaughan identity>.

= Prime omega function
{title2=$\omega(n)$}
{parent=Arithmetic function}
{wiki}

The <prime omega function> $\omega(n)$ counts the distinct <prime factors> of a <positive integer> $n$, so it is the <cardinality> of the <prime support of an integer> $P(n)$. For a <prime factorization> with distinct <prime numbers> $p_1,\ldots,p_r$ and positive exponents,
$$
n=p_1^{a_1}\cdots p_r^{a_r}\quad\Longrightarrow\quad
\omega(n)=r=\sum_{p\mid n}1.
$$
At $n=1$ there are no <prime factors>, so the <empty sum> gives $\omega(1)=0$. The <total number of prime factors> instead gives $\Omega(n)=\sum_i a_i$. https://darijgrinberg.gitlab.io/floor.pdf[Darij Grinberg, MIT 18.781, Floor and arithmetic functions, Definition 2.1.2: distinct and repeated prime factors]{ref}

For $72=2^3\cdot3^2$, the <prime support of an integer> is $\{2,3\}$ and $\omega(72)=2$. The comparison examples give $\omega(30)=3$, $\omega(32)=1$ and $\omega(36)=2$. Raising an existing <prime factor> to a higher power does not affect the <prime omega function>: $\omega(p^k)=1$ for a <prime number> $p$ and $k\ge1$. Thus the <prime omega function> is a <strongly additive arithmetic function>, with
$$
\omega(ab)=\omega(a)+\omega(b)\quad\text{if }\gcd(a,b)=1.
$$
The reason is that <coprime integers> have disjoint <prime supports of an integer>. For arbitrary <positive integers>, their <prime support of an integer> combines by <set union>, and the shared <prime factors> are exactly those of the <greatest common divisor>. Counting this <set union> gives the more general identity
$$
\omega(ab)=\omega(a)+\omega(b)-\omega(\gcd(a,b)).
$$
For $a=12$, $b=18$, both <prime supports of an integer> are $\{2,3\}$ and $\gcd(a,b)=6$, so $\omega(216)=2+2-2=2$. In particular $\omega(4)=1\ne2=\omega(2)+\omega(2)$: the <prime omega function> is not a <completely additive arithmetic function>.

The <prime omega function> is useful for counting choices involving distinct <prime factors>. Every <squarefree divisor> of $n$ chooses a <subset> of its <prime support of an integer>, so there are $2^{\omega(n)}$ <squarefree divisors>. For $72$, this gives $2^2=4$, namely $1,2,3,6$. Also $\omega(\operatorname{rad}(n))=\omega(n)$, because the <radical of an integer> keeps exactly that <prime support of an integer>. The <total number of prime factors> records what this count discards, and the two counts agree exactly on <squarefree integers>.

= Three to the distinct-prime-factor count
{parent=Prime omega function}
{title2=$3^{\omega(n)}$}

The <multiplicative arithmetic function> $f(n)=3^{\omega(n)}$ counts assignments of three labels to each distinct <prime factor>. It also counts pairs of <squarefree divisors> whose <least common multiple> is the <radical of an integer> $n$.

= Summatory bound for three to the prime omega
{parent=Three to the distinct-prime-factor count}

The <Euler product> and <Mertens second theorem> give $\sum_{n\leq D}3^{\omega(n)}/n\ll\log^3D$. The <log-weighted convolution bound for three to the prime omega>, with the <Chebyshev estimate> $\psi(y)\ll y$, then gives $\sum_{\sqrt D\leq n\leq D}3^{\omega(n)}\ll D\log^2D$. A direct bound for the full range follows from $3^{\omega(n)}\leq\tau_3(n)$, where $\tau_3(n)=\#\{(a,b,c):abc=n\}$:
$$
\sum_{n\leq D}3^{\omega(n)}\leq\sum_{abc\leq D}1\leq D\sum_{a\leq D}\frac1a\sum_{b\leq D}\frac1b\ll D\log^2D.
$$

= Log-weighted convolution bound for three to the prime omega
{parent=Three to the distinct-prime-factor count}

For $f(n)=3^{\omega(n)}$,
$$
f(n)\log n\leq3(f*\Lambda)(n).
$$
If $p^a\Vert n$, its contribution to $(f*\Lambda)(n)$ is $f(n)(a-1+1/3)\log p$. Since $3(a-1+1/3)\geq a$, summing over the <prime factors> proves the bound. Here $*$ is <Dirichlet convolution> and $\Lambda$ is the <Von Mangoldt function>.

= Möbius function
{c}
{title2=$\mu(n)$}
{parent=Arithmetic function}
{wiki}

The <Möbius function> assigns a cancellation weight to each <positive integer>:
$$
\mu(n)=\begin{cases}
1&n=1,\\
(-1)^r&n=p_1\cdots p_r\text{ with distinct primes }p_1,\ldots,p_r,\\
0&p^2\mid n\text{ for some prime }p.
\end{cases}
$$
Thus a <squarefree integer> contributes a sign determined by its number of distinct <prime factors>; a repeated <prime factor> makes the weight zero. This packages the alternating signs of <inclusion-exclusion> into an <arithmetic function>. https://darijgrinberg.gitlab.io/floor.pdf[Darij Grinberg, MIT 18.781 lecture notes, Definition 2.1.2]{ref}

For example, $\mu(2)=\mu(3)=-1$, $\mu(6)=1$, and $\mu(30)=(-1)^3=-1$, since $30=2\cdot3\cdot5$. In contrast, $\mu(4)=\mu(12)=\mu(60)=0$: all three have the square of the <prime number> $2$ as a <divisor>. On <prime powers>, $\mu(p)=-1$ and $\mu(p^a)=0$ for $a\geq2$.

The <Möbius function> is a <multiplicative function>: for <coprime integers>, their <prime factors> are disjoint, so either a squared <prime factor> forces both sides of $\mu(mn)=\mu(m)\mu(n)$ to vanish, or the counts of distinct <prime factors> add and their signs multiply. It is not a <completely multiplicative function>, since $\mu(4)=0\ne1=\mu(2)^2$. Its <Möbius divisor-sum identity> makes it the inverse of the <constant-one arithmetic function> under <Dirichlet convolution>: $\mu*\mathbf1=\varepsilon$, the <identity for Dirichlet convolution>. This is why the small set of weights $-1,0,1$ can undo an entire <divisor sum>.

= Möbius divisor-sum identity
{c}
{parent=Möbius function}
{title2=$\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(d)=1_{n=1}$}

For every <positive integer> $n$, the <divisor sum> of the <Möbius function> is
$$
\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(d)=\begin{cases}1&n=1,\\0&n>1.\end{cases}
$$
The sum includes all positive <integer divisors>, but only <squarefree divisors> contribute. Equivalently, $\mu*\mathbf1=\varepsilon$ under <Dirichlet convolution>, where $\mathbf1$ is the <constant-one arithmetic function> and $\varepsilon$ is the <identity for Dirichlet convolution>. https://darijgrinberg.gitlab.io/floor.pdf[Darij Grinberg, MIT 18.781 lecture notes, Propositions 2.4.6 and 2.4.7]{ref}

Each <squarefree divisor> chooses a <subset> of the distinct <prime factors> of $n$, contributing $(-1)^r$ when that <subset> has $r$ elements. Expanding a product therefore gives
$$
\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(d)=\prod_{p\mid n}(1-1).
$$
For $n>1$ at least one <prime factor> occurs, so the product is zero. At $n=1$ it is an <empty product>, equal to one. This is the same alternating cancellation that drives <inclusion-exclusion>.

For $30=2\cdot3\cdot5$, all eight positive <integer divisors> are <squarefree integers>, giving
$$
\mu(1)+\mu(2)+\mu(3)+\mu(5)+\mu(6)+\mu(10)+\mu(15)+\mu(30)
=1-1-1-1+1+1+1-1=0.
$$
Pair each <subset> not containing $2$ with the <subset> obtained by inserting $2$: $(1,2),(3,6),(5,10),(15,30)$. Each pair cancels. The same nonzero terms occur for $60$, because its <squarefree divisors> are still the <integer divisors> of $30$; its other <integer divisors> have <Möbius function> value zero.

\Image[mobius-divisor-cancellation.png]
{title=The eight <integer divisors> of 30 form a <cube> of <subsets> of its <prime factors>; adding one <prime factor> reverses the <Möbius function> sign, and all contributions cancel}

The <Möbius divisor-sum identity> is the cancellation behind <Möbius inversion> and reciprocal <Dirichlet series>: aggregation over <integer divisors> has an exact inverse.

= Möbius harmonic logarithmic moments
{parent=Möbius function}
{c}
{title2=$A_j(x)$}

Put $A_j(x)=\sum_{d\leq x}\mu(d)\log^j(x/d)/d$. The elementary estimates are
$$
A_0(x)=O(1),\qquad A_1(x)=O(1),\qquad A_2(x)=2\log x+O(1).
$$
The identities $\sum_d\mu(d)\lfloor x/d\rfloor=1$ and $\sum_d\mu(d)H_{\lfloor x/d\rfloor}/d=1$ give the first two bounds. Convolve the <harmonic divisor sum> with $\mu(d)/d$: because $\mu*\tau=\mathbf1$, its left side is $H_{\lfloor x\rfloor}$. Thus $\tfrac12A_2+2\gamma A_1+cA_0=\log x+O(1)$. The summed error is $O(x^{-1/2}\sum_{d\leq x}d^{-1/2}\log(2x/d))=O(1)$.

This elementary smoothing argument is used in https://www.math.lsu.edu/~mahlburg/teaching/handouts/2014-7230/Selberg-ElemPNT1949.pdf[Selberg's original proof] and https://ramare-olivier.github.io/Maths/LecturesEasyChennai.pdf[Ramaré's sieve lectures, Lemma 4.2].

= Möbius inversion formula
{c}
{parent=Möbius function}
{title2=$g=f*\mathbf1\iff f=\mu*g$}
{wiki}

= Möbius inversion
{c}
{synonym}

<Möbius inversion> recovers an <arithmetic function> from its <divisor sum>. For <arithmetic functions> $f,g$, the following statements are equivalent, with each equality required for every <positive integer> $n$:
$$
g(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}f(d)
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
f(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(d)g(n/d).
$$
Here $d$ ranges over positive <integer divisors> of $n$ and $\mu$ is the <Möbius function>. No <multiplicativity> assumption is needed, and both sums are finite. The right side can also be written $\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(n/d)g(d)$ by exchanging complementary <integer divisors>. https://darijgrinberg.gitlab.io/floor.pdf[Darij Grinberg, MIT 18.781 lecture notes, Theorem 2.5.1 and Proposition 2.5.2]{ref}

The <divisor sum> $g(n)$ bundles $f(n)$ together with values at smaller <integer divisors>. <Möbius inversion> subtracts aggregates indexed by one distinct <prime factor>, adds those indexed by two, and continues with the alternating signs of <inclusion-exclusion>. A <divisor> containing a squared <prime factor> receives weight zero. The <Möbius divisor-sum identity> ensures that every unwanted contribution cancels exactly.

In <Dirichlet convolution> notation, $g=f*\mathbf1$ and $\mu*\mathbf1=\varepsilon$, where $\mathbf1$ is the <constant-one arithmetic function> and $\varepsilon$ is the <identity for Dirichlet convolution>. <Associativity> and <commutativity> give the short proof:
$$
\mu*g=\mu*(\mathbf1*f)=(\mu*\mathbf1)*f=\varepsilon*f=f.
$$
Convolving $f=\mu*g$ with $\mathbf1$ proves the reverse implication. At $n=1$, the formula reduces to $f(1)=g(1)$ because $\mu(1)=1$.

For a worked example, take $f(n)=\varphi(n)$, <Euler's totient function>. The <totient divisor-sum identity> says that $g(n)=n$, the <identity arithmetic function>:
$$
g(12)=\varphi(1)+\varphi(2)+\varphi(3)+\varphi(4)+\varphi(6)+\varphi(12)
=1+1+2+2+2+4=12.
$$
This identity comes from classifying the <integers> $1,\ldots,n$ by their <greatest common divisor> with $n$: the group with $\gcd(j,n)=h$ has $\varphi(n/h)$ members, since $j/h$ and $n/h$ are <coprime integers>. Summing the group sizes gives $n$, as detailed under <totient divisor-sum identity>.

Now undo this aggregation with the <Möbius function>:
$$
\begin{aligned}
\varphi(12)&=\mu(1)12+\mu(2)6+\mu(3)4+\mu(4)3+\mu(6)2+\mu(12)1\\
&=12-6-4+0+2+0=4.
\end{aligned}
$$
The nonzero terms use $d=1,2,3,6$; $d=4,12$ vanish because they contain the square of the <prime number> $2$. The four surviving <integers> <coprime> to $12$ are $1,5,7,11$.

\Image[mobius-inversion-totient.png]
{title=A <divisor sum> aggregates <Euler's totient function> values to 12, while <Möbius inversion> recovers the four <integers> <coprime> to 12}

This example also reveals why <Möbius inversion> is useful: it turns an easy count of all objects into a count satisfying a <coprimality> restriction. Expanding over <subsets> of distinct <prime factors> gives
$$
\varphi(n)=\sum_{d\mid n}\mu(d)\frac nd
=n\prod_{p\mid n}\left(1-\frac1p\right).
$$
The product is <inclusion-exclusion> over multiples of the <prime factors>. For $12$, it gives $12(1-1/2)(1-1/3)=4$. More generally, whenever a counting problem produces a <divisor sum>, <Möbius inversion> lets that aggregate be reversed without solving a new system of <equations>.

= Mertens function
{c}
{title2=$M(x)$}
{parent=Möbius function}
{wiki}

The Mertens function is the summatory <Möbius function>
$$
M(x)=\sum_{n\leq x}\mu(n).
$$

= Prime number asymptotic from Mertens cancellation
{parent=Mertens function}
{title2=$M(x)=o(x)\Longrightarrow\psi(x)=x+o(x)$}

Let $\Delta(N)=\sum_{n\le N}(\tau(n)-\log n-2\gamma)$. The <Möbius divisor-sum identity> and the <Von Mangoldt divisor identity> give $\mu*\tau=\mathbf1$ and $\mu*\log=\Lambda$, so
$$
\psi(N)=N-2\gamma-\sum_{d\le N}\mu(d)\Delta(\lfloor N/d\rfloor).
$$
Here $*$ is <Dirichlet convolution>. The <divisor summatory asymptotic> bounds the terms with $d\le N/K$ in absolute value by $O(N/\sqrt K)$. For $d>N/K$, group by $j=\lfloor N/d\rfloor<K$: the remaining sum is $\sum_{j<K}\Delta(j)(M(N/j)-M(N/(j+1)))=o_K(N)$. First let $N$ tend to infinity, then $K$ tend to infinity. This proves the <second Chebyshev function> asymptotic from cancellation of the <Mertens function>.

= Mertens bound from a log-integrable Chebyshev error
{c}
{parent=Mertens function}
{title2=$M(x)\ll x/\log x$}

Suppose $\Psi(x)=x+O(xE(x))$ and $\sum_{j\geq0}\sup_{2^j\leq u<2^{j+1}}|E(u)|<\infty$. The <Möbius divisor-sum identity> gives $\sum_{d\leq x}\mu(d)/d=O(1)$. The exact <Dirichlet convolution> identity $\mu\log=-\mu*\Lambda$ then gives $\sum_{n\leq x}\mu(n)\log n=O(x)$, because its error is a convergent dyadic harmonic sum. Since $\sum_{n\leq x}\log(x/n)=O(x)$, the displayed <Mertens function> bound follows. The classical <prime number theorem> error $E(x)=e^{-c\sqrt{\log x}}$ meets the summability condition. This is a deduction from the Chebyshev error itself, without a separate estimate for the reciprocal <zeta function>.

= Mertens function bound from the classical zeta zero-free region
{c}
{parent=Mertens function}

The classical <zero-free region of the Riemann zeta function> and a <truncated Perron formula> give
$$
M(x)\ll x\exp(-c\sqrt{\log x})
$$
for some absolute constant $c>0$. Take contour height $T=\exp(\sqrt{\log x})$ and move the Perron contour to $\Re s=1-c_1/\log T$.

\Include[analytic-number-theory]{parent=Number theory}

= Exponentiation by squaring
{parent=Number theory}
{wiki}
{title2=$a^n$}

= Repeated squaring
{synonym}

Exponentiation by squaring computes an <integer> power by reading the exponent's <binary expansion>. Appending a <binary digit> $b$ to a prefix of value $p$ replaces $p$ by $2p+b$, so the corresponding power changes by
$$
a^{2p+b}=(a^p)^2a^b,\qquad b\in\{0,1\}.
$$
Each new digit requires one squaring and, only for digit one, one further <multiplication>. The number of digits grows with the <logarithm> of the exponent, making this useful for <modular exponentiation>, <primality testing>, and powers of <matrices> using <matrix multiplication>.<A Computational Introduction to Number Theory and Algebra by Victor Shoup>[Section 3.4, pp. 65–66: binary-prefix recurrence and repeated-squaring algorithm; p. 67: primality-testing application]{ref}

For a concrete calculation, $13=(1101)_2=8+4+1$, so $3^{13}=3^8\cdot3^4\cdot3$. The **optimized left-to-right <algorithm> used here** instead keeps one accumulated power: initialize $r=3$ from the leading digit one, then process only the remaining digits $1,0,1$:
$$
\begin{aligned}
1&:\quad r=3,\\
11&:\quad r=3^2\cdot3=27,\\
110&:\quad r=27^2=729,\\
1101&:\quad r=729^2\cdot3=531441\cdot3=1594323.
\end{aligned}
$$
Thus **$3^{13}=1594323$**, using three squarings and two further <multiplications>, instead of twelve successive <multiplications> starting at $3$.

\Image[binary-powering-prefix-and-modular-trace.png]
{title=Reading the same <binary expansion> computes $3^{13}$ and $2^{13}\bmod17$}
{description=Each appended <binary digit> changes the prefix value by $p\mapsto2p+b$. The ordinary-power row grows to $1594323$; the <modular exponentiation> row stores only remainders $2,8,13,15$. Both traces use three squarings and two further <multiplications>.}

For a general positive <integer> $n=(b_{\ell-1}\cdots b_0)_2$ with leading digit $b_{\ell-1}=1$, set $r=a$ and, for $i=\ell-2,\ldots,0$, first replace $r$ by $r^2$, then multiply it by $a$ if $b_i=1$. Return $r$. This explicitly skips the leading digit's otherwise unnecessary operations on one. <A Computational Introduction to Number Theory and Algebra by Victor Shoup> (Section 3.4, pp. 65–66) presents the version initialized at one and processing every digit; the optimization here makes the counting convention unambiguous.

The <loop invariant> is **$r=a^p$, where $p$ is the value of the digits already read**. Initially $p=1$ and $r=a$. If the next digit is $b$, then squaring and optionally multiplying give $r'=a^{2p+b}$, exactly the next prefix. At termination $p=n$. This proof uses <associativity> of <multiplication>; it works in any <monoid>, including <matrices> with the <identity matrix> as <identity element>, and needs no <commutativity> between different bases.

For $n=0$, return the <identity element> without performing a <multiplication>. This is the empty-product convention $a^0=1$; if $a=0$, the <algorithm> therefore returns one for $0^0$ by convention, rather than resolving its meaning in every mathematical setting. Negative <integer> exponents require an <inverse element>: compute $(a^{-1})^{-n}$, counting the inversion separately. Over the <integers>, such an inverse generally leaves the <integers>, and a zero base has no inverse.

= Binary powering multiplication count
{parent=Exponentiation by squaring}
{title2=$C(n)=\ell+\operatorname{wt}(n)-2$}

For the optimized left-to-right <exponentiation by squaring> <algorithm>, let $\ell=\lfloor\log_2 n\rfloor+1$ be the length of the <binary expansion> of a positive <integer> $n$, and let $w=\operatorname{wt}(n)$ be its <Hamming weight>, the number of digits equal to one. There are exactly $\ell-1$ digits after the leading one, and exactly $w-1$ remaining ones. Therefore the count is
$$
S(n)=\ell-1,\qquad T(n)=w-1,\qquad C(n)=S(n)+T(n)=\ell+w-2.
$$
Here $S$ counts squarings and $T$ counts further <multiplications> by the base; **every squaring counts as one <multiplication> in $C$**. Assignment, digit reading, and any inversion are outside this count. The formula includes $n=1$, which requires zero <multiplications>; define $C(0)=0$ separately.

For a fixed length $\ell$, the bounds are $\ell-1\leq C(n)\leq2\ell-2$. A <power of two> reaches the lower bound because its <Hamming weight> is one; $n=2^\ell-1$ reaches the upper bound because all digits are one. For $n=13$, $\ell=4$ and $w=3$, giving $C(13)=5$. Thus the <arithmetic operation> count is $O(\log(n+1))$ in <Big O notation>, but inputs of similar magnitude can have different counts.

\Image[binary-powering-multiplication-count.png]
{title=Exact <multiplication> counts for optimized <exponentiation by squaring> depend on binary length and <Hamming weight>}
{description=For positive exponents through $63$, dots show $C(n)=\ell+\operatorname{wt}(n)-2$, including each squaring. The dashed curves bound all counts at each binary length. Successive multiplication needs $n-1$ operations. The second panel compares squarings and further <multiplications> for exponents $8,13,15,16,31$. These are exact counts, not timing measurements.}

This is a count for the specified <algorithm>, not a claim that it always minimizes <multiplications> among all methods. For instance, at $n=15$ the count is six, but the powers with exponents $1,2,3,6,12,15$ can be built in five <multiplications>: square, multiply by $a$, square, square, then multiply by the already stored $a^3$. Saving operations this way uses a different schedule and stored intermediate powers.

= Modular exponentiation
{parent=Exponentiation by squaring}
{title2=$a^b\bmod m$}

Modular exponentiation computes the <remainder> $a^n\bmod m$ for an <integer> base $a$, a <nonnegative integer> exponent $n$, and, here, an <integer> modulus $m\geq2$. It applies <exponentiation by squaring> while reducing after **each** <multiplication>, including each squaring. The result is the unique representative in $\{0,\ldots,m-1\}$. There is no requirement that the base be <coprime> to the modulus when the exponent is nonnegative.<A Computational Introduction to Number Theory and Algebra by Victor Shoup>[Section 3.4, pp. 64–66: canonical representatives, modular products, and nonnegative-exponent repeated squaring]{ref}

Take $a=2$, $n=13=(1101)_2$, and $m=17$. Initialize $r=2$; the remaining digits give
$$
\begin{aligned}
11&:\quad 2^2\bmod17=4,\quad4\cdot2\bmod17=8,\\
110&:\quad 8^2\bmod17=64\bmod17=13,\\
1101&:\quad13^2\bmod17=169\bmod17=16,\quad16\cdot2\bmod17=15.
\end{aligned}
$$
Hence **$2^{13}\bmod17=15$**. The final operation multiplies $16$ by $2$, since the preceding square has already been reduced. The computation never needs to construct $2^{13}=8192$.

In general reduce the base first, $x=a\bmod m$. For $n>0$, initialize $r=x$ and process the remaining <binary digits> with $r\leftarrow r^2\bmod m$, followed, for a digit one, by $r\leftarrow rx\bmod m$. The <loop invariant> is
$$
0\leq r<m,\qquad r\equiv a^p\pmod m,
$$
where $p$ is the processed prefix value. The congruence is preserved because <modular arithmetic> respects <multiplication>: if $u\equiv v\pmod m$, then $u^2\equiv v^2$ and $ux\equiv va\pmod m$. Thus changing representatives never changes the final <congruence class>.

Both factors in every product are less than $m$, so the unreduced product is less than $m^2$, and the stored result is again less than $m$. The same bounds hold for a gigantic exponent, such as $n=2^{1000}$: there are $1000$ squarings, each on bounded-size residues. For base two, the corresponding ordinary power would have $2^{1000}+1$ <binary digits>. The benefit is avoiding the huge ordinary power altogether, rather than constructing it and taking one final <remainder>. Initial reduction is essential when the supplied base is much larger than the modulus.

For $n=0$, return $1$ under the same empty-product convention as <exponentiation by squaring>. For $n=-k<0$, require $\gcd(a,m)=1$, find the <modular multiplicative inverse> $a^{-1}\bmod m$, and compute $(a^{-1})^k\bmod m$; the <extended Euclidean algorithm> can find that inverse. Its cost is separate from the positive-exponent <binary powering multiplication count>. For example, $2^{-1}\equiv9\pmod{17}$, while $2^{-1}\bmod4$ is undefined because $2$ and $4$ are not <coprime>.

A <reversible computation> gives the coherent <quantum modular exponentiation> used in <quantum order finding> and <discrete-logarithm Fourier sampling>.

= Bit cost of modular exponentiation
{parent=Modular exponentiation}
{title2=$O(\ell L^2)$}

The <binary powering multiplication count> counts <arithmetic operations>. The <time complexity> measured in operations on individual <bits> also depends on operand lengths. Put $L=\lfloor\log_2m\rfloor+1$, the binary length of the modulus, and let $\ell$ be the positive exponent's binary length. Stored residues use at most $L$ <bits>, and an unreduced product uses at most $2L$ <bits>.

With ordinary long <multiplication> and <division with remainder>, one modular product costs $O(L^2)$ operations on <bits>. Combining that cost with $O(\ell)$ modular products gives
$$
\text{bit cost of the powering loop}=O(\ell L^2).
$$
This bound includes reductions in the loop, and assumes the base has already been reduced and the exponent is available as a <binary expansion>. Reading the exponent takes $O(\ell)$ additional work; initial reduction of a very long base depends on its input length, and an inverse for a negative exponent adds further work. More generally, if a modular product costs $R(L)$ operations on <bits>, the powering loop costs $O(\ell R(L))$. Faster <multiplication> and reduction methods can improve the quadratic bound.<A Computational Introduction to Number Theory and Algebra by Victor Shoup>[Section 3.3.5, pp. 62–63, Theorem 3.3: operand-length costs; Section 3.4, p. 65: modular multiplication and powering bounds; Section 3.5, pp. 69–71: faster arithmetic]{ref}

For ordinary <integer> powers with $|a|\geq2$, the output itself has $\lfloor n\log_2|a|\rfloor+1$ <binary digits>. Even writing it takes work proportional to that length. Consequently $O(\log(n+1))$ <multiplications> does not imply $O(\log(n+1))$ operations on <bits>: the operands grow during ordinary <exponentiation by squaring>, whereas <modular exponentiation> keeps them bounded by the modulus. A squaring or multiplication by a small fixed base can also cost less than a general product, so equal counts need not mean equal running times.

= Integer
{title2=$\mathbb Z$}
{parent=Number theory}
{wiki}

An integer is a whole number, positive, negative, or zero.

= Repdigit
{parent=Integer}
{title2=$R_n=d\frac{b^n-1}{b-1}$}
{wiki}

A <repdigit> is a positive integer whose base-$b$ expansion repeats one nonzero digit $d$. For $b\ge2$, $1\le d<b$ and $n\ge1$, the <finite geometric series> gives $R_n=d(1+b+\cdots+b^{n-1})=d(b^n-1)/(b-1)$. For example, $5(10^n-1)/9$ has $n$ decimal digits, all equal to five.

Every <prime number> not dividing $b$ divides infinitely many such <repdigits>. If it divides $d$, every length works. Otherwise, if it does not divide $b-1$, the <Fermat little theorem> gives divisibility for lengths that are multiples of $p-1$, and division by $b-1$ is justified by its <modular inverse>. If $p\mid b-1$, the sum is congruent to $dn$ modulo $p$, so lengths divisible by $p$ work instead. A prime dividing $b$ but not $d$ divides none, since $R_n\equiv d\pmod p$. This distinction prevents invalid cancellation of the geometric-sum denominator.

= Euclidean division
{parent=Integer}
{c}
{title2=$a=bq+r,\quad0\le r<b$}
{wiki}

= Division with remainder
{synonym}

For an <integer> $a$ and positive <integer> $b$, Euclidean division writes $a=bq+r$ with unique integer quotient $q$ and remainder $0\le r<b$. For $a\ge0$, repeated quotient-and-remainder steps yield the digits of a <base-p expansion> when $b=p$. The uniqueness follows because the difference of two permitted remainders cannot be a nonzero multiple of $b$.

= Remainder
{parent=Euclidean division}
{title2=$r=a-bq,\quad0\leq r<b$}
{wiki}

For an <integer> $a$ and positive <integer> $b$, the remainder in <Euclidean division> is the unique <integer> $r$ in $0\leq r<b$ for which $a=bq+r$ with an <integer> $q$. It is not the full <quotient> $a/b$.

= Long division
{parent=Euclidean division}
{wiki}

Repeated <division with remainder> that determines successive digits of a <quotient>. To generate the fractional digits in base $b$, multiply the current <remainder> by $b$, divide by the <denominator>, and keep the resulting <remainder> for the next step.

= Integer interval
{parent=Integer}
{title2=$[a,b]\cap\mathbb Z$}

A finite <integer interval> is a set $\{a,a+1,\ldots,b\}$ with <integers> $a\leq b$. Its length is $b-a+1$. Infinite <integer intervals> can also be defined by allowing one or both endpoints to be infinite; a bounded-gaps condition on words always quantifies over finite <integer intervals>.

= Positional notation
{parent=Integer}
{wiki}

In positional notation of base $b$, a digit string $d_nd_{n-1}\cdots d_0$ represents $\sum_{j=0}^nd_jb^j$.

= Decimal expansion
{parent=Positional notation}
{title2=$M=\sum_{i=0}^s a_i10^i$}

A nonnegative integer's <decimal expansion> uses digits $0\leq a_i\leq9$. Testing divisibility reduces this polynomial in ten modulo the divisor: for example $10\equiv-1\pmod{11}$ makes divisibility by eleven equivalent to vanishing of the alternating digit sum.

= Hereditary base representation
{parent=Positional notation}
{title2=$m=\sum_i b^{e_i}c_i$}

A <hereditary base representation> expands not only the number in base $b$, but also every exponent recursively in that same base. Changing the base at every depth defines the Goodstein base-change operation; replacing it by omega defines an <ordinal rank of a Goodstein term>.

= Goodstein sequence
{parent=Hereditary base representation}
{c}
{title2=$m_{s+1}=B_{s+2,s+3}(m_s)-1$}

Start at base two. At each nonzero step, change every base occurrence in the <hereditary base representation> to the next base and subtract one. Although the natural-number terms can grow, their <ordinal ranks of a Goodstein term> decrease strictly. Keep zero fixed once it is reached.

= Goodstein's theorem
{parent=Goodstein sequence}
{c}
{wiki}

Every <Goodstein sequence> reaches zero. Before zero, the <ordinal rank of a Goodstein term> of each step is strictly smaller than the preceding one. An infinite <sequence> would contradict <well-foundedness> of the <ordinals> below <epsilon zero>.

= Ordinal rank of a Goodstein term
{parent=Goodstein sequence}
{title2=$\rho_b(m)$}

= Ordinal ranks of a Goodstein term
{synonym}

Replacing every base occurrence by omega in a <hereditary base representation> gives a <Cantor normal form> below <epsilon zero>. Hereditary base change preserves this <ordinal> expression, and subtraction of one lowers it strictly. The <ordinal> is a termination measure even when the natural-number value increases.

= Goodstein function
{parent=Goodstein sequence}
{c}
{title2=$\mathcal G(m)=\min\{s:m_s=0\}$}

The <Goodstein function> records the least step at which the <Goodstein sequence> from a given initial number reaches zero. With the initial step numbered zero, its values on $0,1,2,3$ are $0,1,3,5$. <Goodstein's theorem> makes this effective termination-time <function> total.

= Base-p expansion
{parent=Positional notation}
{title2=$k=\sum_{i\ge0}k_ip^i,\quad0\le k_i<p$}

For an integer base $p\ge2$, a nonnegative <integer> has a finite positional representation with digits $k_i\in\{0,\ldots,p-1\}$. Repeated <Euclidean division> gives the digits uniquely; appending zero high-place digits has no effect. The base need not be prime for uniqueness, but a prime base is useful in <Lucas theorem> and modular <binomial coefficients>.

= Binary numeral system
{parent=Positional notation}
{wiki}

The binary numeral system is <positional notation> of base two, using the digits zero and one.

= Binary digit
{parent=Binary numeral system}
{title2=$b_j\in\{0,1\},\quad n=\sum_jb_j2^j$}

A <binary digit> is a zero or one in the base-two positional representation of an <integer>. The sum $s_2(n)=\sum_jb_j$ determines the <Thue–Morse sequence> and is the <Hamming weight> of the binary representation.

= Decimal
{parent=Positional notation}
{wiki}

The decimal numeral system is <positional notation> in base ten.

= Decimal representation
{parent=Decimal}

A decimal representation is a <positional notation> of base ten.

= Terminating decimal
{parent=Decimal representation}

A <decimal representation> with only finitely many nonzero fractional digits. A <reduced fraction> has this property exactly when its <denominator> has no <prime factors> other than two and five. The zero tail is distinguished from the genuine repeating block measured by <decimal period from multiplicative order>.

= Positive integer
{parent=Integer}

A positive integer is an <integer> greater than zero.

= Power of two
{parent=Positive integer}
{title2=$2^k,\quad k\in\mathbb Z_{\geq0}$}

= Powers of two
{synonym}

In this <positive integer> context, a <power of two> is $2^k$ for a <nonnegative integer> $k$, giving $1,2,4,8,\ldots$. Allowing negative or real <exponents> gives a broader meaning of <exponentiation>, which is not intended in the integer divisibility criteria here.

= Perfect number
{parent=Positive integer}
{title2=$s(n)=n$}
{wiki}

A <positive integer> whose <proper-divisor sum> equals itself; equivalently, its <sum-of-divisors function> satisfies $\sigma(n)=2n$. For example, $28=1+2+4+7+14$.

= Deficient number
{parent=Positive integer}
{title2=$s(n)<n$}
{wiki}

A <positive integer> whose <proper-divisor sum> is smaller than itself; equivalently, its <sum-of-divisors function> satisfies $\sigma(n)<2n$. For example, $s(8)=7<8$; also $s(1)=0<1$.

= Abundant number
{parent=Positive integer}
{title2=$s(n)>n$}
{wiki}

A <positive integer> whose <proper-divisor sum> exceeds itself; equivalently, its <sum-of-divisors function> satisfies $\sigma(n)>2n$. For example, $s(12)=16>12$.

= Parity
{disambiguate=mathematics}
{parent=Integer}
{wiki}

= Integer parity
{synonym}

The parity of an <integer> records whether it is divisible by two.

= Odd integer
{parent=Parity (mathematics)}

An odd integer is not divisible by two; equivalently, it has the form $2k+1$.

= Even number
{parent=Parity (mathematics)}

= Even integer
{synonym}

An integer is even when it is divisible by two.

= Odd number
{parent=Parity (mathematics)}

An integer is odd when it is not divisible by two.

= Square-free integer
{parent=Integer}
{wiki}

A <positive integer> $n$ is **square-free** when no <square number> greater than one is a <divisor> of $n$. It suffices to exclude squares of <prime numbers>; equivalently, every <prime valuation> is zero or one:
$$
n\text{ is square-free}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
p^2\nmid n\text{ for every prime }p
\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad v_p(n)\in\{0,1\}\text{ for every prime }p.
$$
Thus the <prime factorization> of a <square-free integer> is a <product> of distinct <prime factors>, with no repetitions. One is included: its <prime factorization> is an <empty product> and all its <prime valuations> are zero. These equivalent tests are stated in Grinberg's notes. https://darijgrinberg.gitlab.io/floor.pdf[Darij Grinberg, MIT 18.781, Floor and arithmetic functions, Definition 2.1.2 and footnote 6]{ref}

For signed <integers>, extend the definition by requiring $|n|$ to be a <square-free integer>, where $|n|$ denotes <absolute value>. Hence $-30$ and both $1,-1$ qualify. Zero does not: every <prime number> has its square as a <divisor> of zero. The <Möbius function> and <radical of an integer> below use their existing <positive integer> domains, so apply them to $|n|$ when handling a negative <integer>.

For example, $30=2\cdot3\cdot5$ is a <square-free integer>, whereas $12=2^2\cdot3$ is not, because $4\mid12$ and $v_2(12)=2$. Being a <composite number> causes no problem: $6,10,15$ are all <square-free integers>. A <square-free integer> need not be <coprime> to another <integer>: $6$ and $10$ are both <square-free integers> but have <greatest common divisor> two. In fact the only signed <integers> <coprime> to every <integer> are $1,-1$, since $\gcd(n,n)=|n|$.

\Image[coprime-squarefree-integers-square-sieve.png]
{title=Exact <square-free integer> classification of the <integers> from one to sixty}
{description=Green cells are <square-free integers>; each red cell displays a <prime number> square that divides its label, choosing the smallest such <prime number>. Only $4,9,25,49$ need testing in this range. There are $37$ green cells. This original finite arithmetic diagram is an exact enumeration, not a measurement or an assertion about a limiting frequency.}

For <positive integers> $a,b$, the useful <product> criterion is
$$
ab\text{ is square-free}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad
a,b\text{ are square-free and }\gcd(a,b)=1.
$$
The proof outline is just <addition> of <prime valuations>: $v_p(ab)=v_p(a)+v_p(b)$ can stay at most one only if neither input repeats a <prime factor> and no <prime factor> occurs in both. Thus $6\cdot5=30$ is a <square-free integer>, but $6\cdot10=60$ is not. An equivalent characterization is that **every** factorization $n=ab$ into <positive integers> has <coprime> factors. If $p^2\mid n$, the factorization $n=p(n/p)$ supplies two factors sharing $p$; conversely, a shared <prime factor> forces its square to divide $n$. For $30$, the unordered factor pairs $(1,30),(2,15),(3,10),(5,6)$ all have <greatest common divisor> one; for $12$, the pair $(2,6)$ fails.

Two existing <arithmetic functions> turn the definition into practical tests:
$$
\operatorname{rad}(n)=n\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad n\text{ is square-free},\qquad
\mu(n)^2=\begin{cases}1&n\text{ is square-free},\\0&\text{otherwise},\end{cases}\qquad n>0.
$$
The <radical of an integer> keeps one copy of each <prime factor>, so it changes precisely the <integers> with a repeated <prime factor>. Its definition is given in Evertse's exercise sheet. https://pub.math.leidenuniv.nl/~evertsejh/dio12-ex4.pdf[Jan-Hendrik Evertse, Diophantine approximation, Homework IV, Exercise 20]{ref} The <Möbius function> assigns $(-1)^r$ to a <square-free integer> with $r$ distinct <prime factors> and zero otherwise; squaring forgets the sign. Consequently $\sum_{n=1}^{60}\mu(n)^2=37$ counts exactly the green cells. This connects a restriction on repeated <prime factors> with weighted counting and <inclusion-exclusion>. https://darijgrinberg.gitlab.io/floor.pdf[Darij Grinberg, MIT 18.781, Definition 2.1.2: the Möbius function]{ref}

= Powerful number
{parent=Integer}
{wiki}

= Squarefull number
{synonym}

A positive integer is squarefull when every <prime> dividing it also divides it to at least the second power. Thus $n$ is not squarefull as soon as some prime $p$ satisfies $p\mid n$ but $p^2\nmid n$.

= Square number
{title2=$n^2$}
{parent=Integer}
{wiki}

= Perfect square
{synonym}

A square number is an <integer> of the form $n^2$ for some integer $n$. Consecutive nonnegative squares differ by $(n+1)^2-n^2=2n+1$.

= Divisibility
{title2=$\mid$}
{parent=Number theory}
{wiki}

For <integers> $d,n$, **$d$ divides $n$** when <multiplication> by some <integer> $k$ takes $d$ to $n$:
$$
d\mid n\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad \text{there exists }k\in\mathbb Z\text{ such that }n=dk.
$$
Then $d$ is a <divisor> of $n$, and $n$ is an <integer multiple> of $d$. The notation $d\nmid n$ means that no such <integer> $k$ exists. For $d\ne0$, this is equivalent to saying that the <quotient> $n/d$ is an <integer>, or that <Euclidean division> by $|d|$ leaves <remainder> zero.

For example, $3\mid12$ because $12=3\cdot4$, whereas $5\nmid12$ because $12=5\cdot2+2$. Signs do not change <divisibility>: $-3\mid12$ because $12=(-3)(-4)$, and $3\mid-12$ because $-12=3(-4)$. In general, changing the sign of either input preserves the relation.

The definition here permits zero as either input. Every <integer> divides zero, since $0=d\cdot0$. In particular, **$0\mid0$**; however, $0\nmid n$ whenever $n\ne0$, since $0k$ is always zero. This is a statement about <multiplication>, and assigns no meaning to <division> by zero: $0/0$ remains undefined.

Two useful properties come directly from the defining <multiplication>. <Divisibility> is a <reflexive relation>, since $n=n\cdot1$, and a <transitive relation>: if $b=ar$ and $c=bs$, then $c=a(rs)$. Moreover, a shared <divisor> divides every <integer> <linear combination>:
$$
d\mid a,\quad d\mid b\quad\Longrightarrow\quad d\mid(ax+by)\qquad(x,y\in\mathbb Z).
$$
Indeed, writing $a=du$ and $b=dv$ gives $ax+by=d(ux+vy)$. For example, $6\mid12$ and $6\mid18$ imply $6\mid(18-12)$. This small observation explains why the <Euclidean algorithm> preserves common <divisors>, and why <Bézout's identity> can certify a <greatest common divisor>.

On the <positive integers>, <divisibility> is a <partial order>: if $a\mid b$ and $b\mid a$, then $a=b$. The restriction matters, since $3\mid-3$ and $-3\mid3$ although $3\ne-3$. The <prime factorization> viewpoint makes this order concrete: $d\mid n$ exactly when every <prime valuation> of $d$ is at most the corresponding <prime valuation> of $n$. Thus one relation connects equal grouping, <prime factorization>, and <modular arithmetic>.

Book VII, definitions 3 and 5 of <Euclid's Elements> describe the corresponding positive-number relation through one number measuring another and through <integer multiples>. The sign and zero conventions above belong to the present <integer> formulation, rather than to those ancient definitions. https://geometor.github.io/euclid/heath/VII/index.html[Euclid, Elements VII, definitions 3 and 5, T. L. Heath translation]{ref}

= Integer multiple
{parent=Divisibility}
{title2=$n=dk,\quad k\in\mathbb Z$}

An integer multiple of an <integer> $d$ is a <product> $dk$ with $k$ an <integer>; equivalently, $d$ is a <divisor> of that <product>. The <integer multiples> of $12$ are $\ldots,-24,-12,0,12,24,\ldots$. Zero is an <integer multiple> of every <integer>, and the only <integer multiple> of zero is zero. Shared positive <integer multiples> lead to the <least common multiple>.

= Divisor
{parent=Divisibility}
{title2=$d\mid n$}
{wiki}

= Integer divisor
{synonym}

A divisor of an <integer> $n$ is an <integer> $d$ satisfying the <divisibility> relation $d\mid n$. Equivalently, $n=dk$ for some <integer> $k$. The word **positive** matters when listing or counting <divisors>: unless signs are restricted, both $d$ and $-d$ qualify.

The positive <divisors> of $12$ are
$$
1,2,3,4,6,12.
$$
Their negatives are also <divisors> of $12$, giving twelve <integer divisors> altogether. The same list of positive <divisors> works for $-12$. One has only the positive <divisor> $1$, while zero has every <integer> as a <divisor>, including zero under our <divisibility> convention. A nonzero <integer> never has zero as a <divisor>.

For a <positive integer> $n$, the positive <divisors> come in pairs $(d,n/d)$ whose <product> is $n$. For $12$, the pairs are $(1,12)$, $(2,6)$ and $(3,4)$. Each pair contains a <divisor> at most $\sqrt n$, so searching through candidate <divisors> up to the <square root> suffices to find all pairs. A pair repeats its own member only when $n$ is a <perfect square>; for $36$, the middle pair is $(6,6)$. Consequently a <positive integer> has an odd number of positive <divisors> exactly when it is a <perfect square>.

The <prime factorization> gives another complete description. Since $12=2^2\cdot3$, its positive <divisors> are precisely $2^i3^j$ with $0\leq i\leq2$ and $0\leq j\leq1$. These independent choices give $(2+1)(1+1)=6$ positive <divisors>, the value of the <divisor function> $\tau(12)$. A <prime factor> is one particular kind of <divisor>; $4$, $6$ and $12$ show that <divisors> need not be <prime numbers>.

Thinking in pairs connects exact grouping with <integer factorization>: twelve objects can form three groups of four or four groups of three. Thinking in shared <divisors> gives the <greatest common divisor>; for $12$ and $18$, the positive common <divisors> are $1,2,3,6$, and the largest is $6$.

= Proper divisor
{parent=Divisor}
{title2=$d\mid n,\quad 1\le d<n$}

Here a <proper divisor> of a <positive integer> $n$ means a positive <integer divisor> other than $n$ itself. Thus $1$ has no <proper divisors>, and the <proper divisors> of $12$ are $1,2,3,4,6$.

= Squarefree divisor
{parent=Divisor}

A squarefree divisor of an <integer> $n$ is a <divisor> $d\mid n$ that is a <squarefree integer>. It is specified by choosing a subset of the distinct <prime factors> of $n$.

= Greatest common divisor
{title2=$\gcd$}
{parent=Divisibility}
{wiki}

For <integers> $a,b$ not both zero, their greatest common divisor is the largest positive <integer> dividing both. It is unchanged by replacing either input by its negative. The convention $\gcd(0,0)=0$ is useful, but this exceptional value is not a largest positive common <divisor>. For positive inputs, the <Euclidean algorithm> computes the answer, and <Bézout's identity> expresses it as an integer <linear combination> of the inputs.

For $12=2^2\cdot3$ and $18=2\cdot3^2$, a common <divisor> can contain at most one copy of the <prime number> two and one of the <prime number> three. Taking the smaller <prime valuation> at each <prime number> therefore gives
$$
\gcd(12,18)=2^{\min(2,1)}3^{\min(1,2)}=2\cdot3=6.
$$
The check $6=18-12$ is also a <Bézout's identity>: every common <divisor> divides this difference, and six itself divides both inputs. More generally, every common <divisor> divides the <greatest common divisor>, a stronger statement than merely being no larger than it. The companion <least common multiple> takes the larger <prime valuations>, as the diagram there illustrates. For an application, divide the <numerator> and <denominator> of the <fraction> $12/18$ by six to obtain $2/3$; these remaining <integers> are <coprime>.

= Scaling identity for greatest common divisors
{parent=Greatest common divisor}
{title2=$\gcd(km,kn)=k\gcd(m,n)$}

For positive integers $m,n,k$, the <greatest common divisor> scales by the common multiplier $k$. If $d=\gcd(m,n)$, then $kd$ is a common divisor of $km,kn$. Meanwhile <Bézout's identity> expresses $kd$ as an integer linear combination of $km,kn$, so their <greatest common divisor> divides $kd$. These two comparisons prove the identity without prime factorization.

= Coprime integers
{parent=Greatest common divisor}
{wiki}

= Coprimality
{synonym}

= Coprime
{synonym}

Two <integers> $a,b$ are **coprime** when their <greatest common divisor> is one:
$$
a,b\text{ are coprime}\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\gcd(a,b)=1.
$$
For <positive integers>, this says that the only positive common <divisor> is one. For signed <integers>, use $\gcd(a,b)=\gcd(|a|,|b|)$, with <absolute values>; changing either sign leaves <coprimality> unchanged. With the convention $\gcd(0,0)=0$, zero is <coprime> precisely to $1,-1$, because $\gcd(a,0)=|a|$. Both $1,-1$ are <coprime> to every <integer>. https://darijgrinberg.gitlab.io/floor.pdf[Darij Grinberg, MIT 18.781, Section 1.2: greatest common divisors and coprimality]{ref}

The word concerns shared <prime factors>, not whether either input is a <prime number>. For instance $8=2^3$ and $9=3^2$ are <coprime> <composite numbers>, even though neither is a <square-free integer>. Conversely, $6=2\cdot3$ and $10=2\cdot5$ are <square-free integers> but are not <coprime>. Consecutive <integers> are always <coprime>: any common <divisor> of $n,n+1$ divides their difference one. Thus $14,15$ provide another pair of <coprime> <composite numbers>.

For <positive integers>, let $P(n)$ be the <prime support of an integer>. The equivalent test is
$$
\gcd(a,b)=1\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad P(a)\cap P(b)=\varnothing.
$$
The <set intersection> of the two <prime supports of an integer> must be the <empty set>. Indeed, any positive common <divisor> greater than one has a <prime factor>, and any shared <prime factor> is a common <divisor> greater than one. Hence <coprimality> depends on which <prime factors> occur, rather than on their <prime valuations>; replacing either input by its <radical of an integer> preserves <coprimality>.

For a list of at least two <integers>, distinguish <pairwise coprimality>, requiring every pair to be <coprime>, from <joint coprimality>, requiring only the <greatest common divisor> of the entire list to be one. For example,
$$
\gcd(6,10,15)=1,\qquad
\gcd(6,10)=2,\quad\gcd(6,15)=3,\quad\gcd(10,15)=5.
$$
No <prime factor> belongs to all three <prime supports of an integer>, yet each pair shares one. By contrast, $4,9,25$ have <pairwise coprimality>. The distinction is presented by Poritz under the names pairwise and mutually relatively prime. https://math.libretexts.org/Bookshelves/Combinatorics_and_Discrete_Mathematics/Yet_Another_Introductory_Number_Theory_Textbook_-_Cryptology_Emphasis_(Poritz)/01:_Well-Ordering_and_Division/1.05:_The_Greatest_Common_Divisor[Jonathan A. Poritz, Yet Another Introductory Number Theory Textbook, Section 1.5]{ref}

\Image[coprime-squarefree-integers-prime-sharing.png]
{title=<Prime support of an integer> distinguishes <coprime> pairs, <joint coprimality> and <pairwise coprimality>}
{description=Filled cells mark membership in the <prime support of an integer>; empty cells mean the <prime number> does not divide the row label. The first panel has two <coprime integers>; the middle has <joint coprimality> but fails <pairwise coprimality>; the last has <pairwise coprimality>. The columns exhaust all <prime factors> of these eight row entries. This original finite arithmetic diagram shows exact <divisibility>, not sampled probabilities.}

One use is simplifying <fractions>. For a <fraction> $a/b$ with <integer> entries and $b>0$, divide its <numerator> and <denominator> by $d=\gcd(a,b)$ to obtain a <reduced fraction> with <coprime> entries. For example, $42/30=7/5$ since $d=6$, and $-42/30=-7/5$ uses the same $d$. A further common <divisor> would make $d$ too small, so the reduction is complete. In particular, the <reduced fraction> for zero is $0/1$.

Another use is reversible <modular arithmetic>: for an <integer modulus> $m>1$, an <integer> $a$ has a <modular inverse> precisely when $a,m$ are <coprime>. The proof outline uses <Bézout's identity>: $ua+vm=1$ makes $u$ a <modular inverse>, and any inverse relation $ua-km=1$ forces every common <divisor> to divide one. For example, $3\cdot5-2\cdot7=1$ gives $5^{-1}\equiv3\pmod7$, whereas $6$ has no <modular inverse> modulo $10$. Thus <coprimality> is the exact condition allowing division by a residue in <modular arithmetic>. https://ocw.mit.edu/courses/18-200-principles-of-discrete-applied-mathematics-spring-2024/mit18_200_s24_lec15.pdf[Ankur Moitra, MIT 18.200, Lecture 15, Lemmas 1–2 and modular inverses]{ref}

= Pairwise coprimality
{parent=Coprime integers}
{title2=$\gcd(a_i,a_j)=1\quad(i\ne j)$}

= Pairwise coprime integers
{synonym}

A list of <integers> $a_1,\ldots,a_r$, with $r\ge2$, has <pairwise coprimality> when every two entries in distinct positions are <coprime>. This implies <joint coprimality>. For <positive integers>, it means no <prime factor> occurs in two different entries. For example, $4,9,25$ satisfy this condition.

= Joint coprimality
{parent=Coprime integers}
{title2=$\gcd(a_1,\ldots,a_r)=1$}

= Jointly coprime integers
{synonym}

A list of <integers> $a_1,\ldots,a_r$, with $r\ge2$, has <joint coprimality> when its <greatest common divisor> is one; define that <greatest common divisor> by repeated application of the two-input operation. For <positive integers>, no <prime factor> may divide every entry. Thus $6,10,15$ have <joint coprimality> but fail <pairwise coprimality>. For two entries, the conditions coincide.

= Least common multiple
{title2=$\operatorname{lcm}$}
{parent=Divisibility}
{wiki}

For <positive integers> $a,b$, their least common multiple is the **smallest positive <integer> that is an <integer multiple> of both**. Write it as $\operatorname{lcm}(a,b)$. A positive common <integer multiple> always exists, since $ab$ is one, but it can be larger than necessary.

For example, the positive <integer multiples> of $12$ begin $12,24,36,48,60,72$, while those of $18$ begin $18,36,54,72$. Their first positive coincidence is $36$, so $\operatorname{lcm}(12,18)=36$. Both also divide zero, but the word positive excludes zero from this minimum.
